It feels completely real.\n\nI used to go hiking a lot, and there’s this one bend in the trail where the light comes through the trees in this strange way—sorry, that probably doesn’t matter—but that’s where it happens sometimes, like he’s just behind my shoulder. That’s why I came in. It’s been scaring me.\n\nDoctor: Who is Garrett to you?\n\nPatient: Garrett is… I’m not sure how to explain him cleanly. He was someone important to me. It’s hard because when I try to put it into a simple category, like friend or—sorry, I’m getting stuck on the word—my mind kind of slides sideways.\n\nHe had this way of standing behind me when I was painting, and I still catch myself expecting that, which is ridiculous because the room is different now, the light’s different. I don’t know. He mattered. A lot.\n\nDoctor: When did Garrett die?\n\nPatient: About a month ago. It’s weird, because time since then doesn’t feel normal—like some days feel stretched out, and then I’ll realize I haven’t done something basic, like taken out the recycling, and somehow a whole week has gone by. But… yeah. Garrett died about a month ago, and everything kind of started unraveling from there.\n\nDoctor: Aside from hearing Garrett’s voice, have you had any other experiences that felt real to you but that other people would probably say weren’t there?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s Garrett’s voice. I mean, that’s the thing that feels… not like a thought. It’s so clear, like he’s standing just behind me on the trail or in my painting room.\n\nAnd I know—at least when I’m sitting here talking to you—I know he’s dead. But when it happens, it doesn’t feel like I’m remembering him. It feels real. Like if I turned around fast enough, he’d be there.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed most of the day. I mean… there are stretches where I feel really heavy, like the air is thicker, but then I’ll get distracted by something stupid, like the pattern of dust on a windowsill or whether I left a mug in the studio, and I’m not exactly sad in that moment.\n\n“Nearly every day” is hard for me to measure. Since Garrett died, the days sort of blur together. So… I’m not sure. clinical ideas to Garrett to theater plans, and I felt this pressure in my chest to keep going before the words disappeared.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or funeral before I can catch any of it.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I can’t ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. That was when it started, I think.

Not right away in the way people mean it, like a movie scene or something. It was during that stretch when I was barely sleeping—two, three hours—and I was just going and going, taking on these impossible things like they had to be done immediately. I remember reorganizing a whole set of files at some ridiculous hour because it suddenly felt connected to… I don’t know, to fixing something.

And around then, I started hearing Garrett. His voice. I hadn’t heard him like that before all of that started. I mean, grief is strange—you remember someone’s voice, you can almost replay it—but this wasn’t just missing him in the ordinary way. It was during that wired period, when I felt driven to keep moving.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. Not when things felt normal, if normal is even the right word.

It started during that stretch when I was barely sleeping, when I had all this energy and kept taking on these huge, impossible things like they somehow had to be done right then. That’s when I heard Garrett’s voice. I hadn’t heard him like that before everything shifted.

I mean, I missed him before, obviously, and grief does these strange things where you can remember someone so clearly it almost feels like they’re in the room. But this wasn’t that ordinary missing-him kind of thing. It came with that wired, driven feeling.

It’s hard to separate from just being unable to slow down.\n\nLike, I’d have these moments where I felt very certain about what I should do next, and I’d start moving things around or making plans, but then I’d get caught up in details—like the way the kitchen light looked at 3 a.m., or some article I read years ago that suddenly seemed important. I wasn’t, you know, trying to blow up my life on purpose.\n\nThere were moments where I’d feel like I needed to do something immediately, but then I’d get sidetracked by the reason behind it, like whether it was actually urgent or just felt urgent because it was three in the morning and everything feels… sharper then.\n\nI’m not sure. Not always, but sometimes my thoughts move so fast I can’t keep the thread.\n\nLike I’ll sit down to write one patient note, and then suddenly that becomes five different projects I need to start, and then I’m remembering something from a hike, and then I’m back at the funeral feeling guilty about something I said or didn’t say—and all of that happens before I can even finish the first sentence.\n\nSo when I try to explain it out loud, I can see people kind of… waiting for me to land somewhere. But I’m already three turns past where I started.\n\nDoctor: Since Garrett died, have there been times when you felt unusually energized or keyed up, almost like you needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. There were weeks where I was barely sleeping—maybe two or three hours—and I didn’t feel tired the way I should’ve. I felt wired, like my body was buzzing, like if I lay down my skin was still… moving, almost.\n\nAnd then it flipped. I started crashing, but not in a normal restful way. It’s like I’d sleep hard in pieces, broken up, and still wake up before dawn exhausted and panicky. I’d look at the clock and it would be some awful gray hour, and I’d already feel behind, like the whole day was coming at me too fast.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you barely slept, did you feel unusually confident or powerful?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it powerful. Confident… maybe? It’s hard to separate from just being unable to slow down.\n\nLike, I’d have these moments where I felt very certain about what I should do next, and I’d start moving things around or making plans, but then I’d get caught up in details—like the way the kitchen light looked at 3 a.m., or some article I read years ago that suddenly seemed important. So… I’m not sure I felt “powerful,” exactly. More like I couldn’t tell if my judgment was normal or if I was just running too hot.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks when you barely slept, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive?\n\nPatient: Risky how? I mean… I don’t think I did anything dramatic, if that’s what you mean. I wasn’t, you know, trying to blow up my life on purpose.\n\nThere were moments where I’d feel like I needed to do something immediately, but then I’d get sidetracked by the reason behind it, like whether it was actually urgent or just felt urgent because it was three in the morning and everything feels… sharper then.\n\nI’m not sure. I guess I’d have to think about what counts as impulsive.\n\nDoctor: Before Garrett died, had you ever had a period like that—needing very little sleep and feeling unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Before Garrett? I… I don’t think so, not like that. I mean, I’ve had busy stretches, everyone has—grad school, deadlines, you know, that feeling where you drink too much coffee and convince yourself you’re fine.\n\nBut this was different. After he died, it was weeks where I was sleeping maybe two or three hours and still feeling wired, like my body didn’t know how to shut off. And then later I’d sort of crash into this heavy, broken sleep, but it didn’t help. I’d wake up before dawn already exhausted and panicky. So… before him, no, I don’t remember it being like that.\n\nDoctor: Did you hear Garrett’s voice during the time when you were sleeping only two or three hours a night?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That was when it started, I think.\n\nNot right away in the way people mean it, like a movie scene or something. It was during that stretch when I was barely sleeping—two, three hours—and I was just going and going, taking on these impossible things like they had to be done immediately. I remember reorganizing a whole set of files at some ridiculous hour because it suddenly felt connected to… I don’t know, to fixing something.\n\nAnd around then, I started hearing Garrett. His voice. I hadn’t heard him like that before all of that started. I mean, grief is strange—you remember someone’s voice, you can almost replay it—but this wasn’t just missing him in the ordinary way. It was during that wired period, when I felt driven to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard Garrett’s voice at times when your sleep and energy felt back to normal?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not when things felt normal, if normal is even the right word.\n\nIt started during that stretch when I was barely sleeping, when I had all this energy and kept taking on these huge, impossible things like they somehow had to be done right then. That’s when I heard Garrett’s voice. I hadn’t heard him like that before everything shifted.\n\nI mean, I missed him before, obviously, and grief does these strange things where you can remember someone so clearly it almost feels like they’re in the room. But this wasn’t that ordinary missing-him kind of thing. It came with that wired, driven feeling.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were barely sleeping and felt wired, did you also feel unusually euphoric or unusually irritable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… both, I guess. At first it wasn’t “happy” exactly, but I felt almost lit up, like there was this urgent purpose and I was the only one who could see how important it was.\n\nBut then if anyone questioned me—like even just asking if I should slow down—I’d snap. I mean really snap, like rage came up so fast it scared people. It felt like they were getting in the way of something that had to happen right then.\n\nDoctor: When you heard Garrett’s voice, did it feel connected to that urgent, driven state you were in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it did. I mean, it didn’t feel like a separate thing, if that makes sense. It started in that same stretch where I was barely sleeping and felt like I had to keep going, like there were all these projects that suddenly mattered more than anything.\n\nBefore that, I’d missed Garrett, of course. I’d think about him, remember how he sounded—sometimes grief does that, you know, where a memory gets really vivid. But I hadn’t heard his voice like that before everything got… sped up.
